Van Gogh’s Starry Night shows a real night sky, study suggests

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

For decades, art historians and astronomers have wondered whether the sky of Vincent van Gogh’s The Starry Night shows real stars or imagined ones. A new study argues the stars are real — and that Van Gogh likely saw them a month later than most scholars thought.

The study, published Oct. 22 in Scientific Reports, comes from a team led by Elijah Jeong-hyun Kim, CEO of the South Korean telescope maker SLLAB and a Ph.D. candidate at Kyung Hee University’s School of Space Research in Yongin.

By combining astronomical data, historical primary sources, and statistical analysis, the team narrowed the celestial alignments shown in the painting’s sky to a handful of late-July mornings.

They further suggest Van Gogh likely painted the eastern sky as seen from his asylum room in Saint-Rémy-de-Provence, France, around 3 a.m. on July 22, 1889. That morning, a waning crescent Moon, Venus, and the Hyades — the V-shaped star cluster in Taurus the Bull — were all gathered in the same small patch of sky.

RELATED: Exploring the solar system through space art

In May 1889, Van Gogh admitted himself to the asylum of Saint-Paul-de-Mausole, a former monastery outside Saint-Rémy. The previous December, after a falling-out with fellow painter Paul Gauguin, he had cut off his left ear, and more breakdowns followed. At the asylum he kept working, producing some 150 paintings, including famous works like Almond Blossom and The Starry Night.

On June 18, he wrote to his brother Theo that he had begun “a new study of a starry sky.” For most scholars, that line anchored the painting to the mid-June sky.

In 1984, art historian Albert Boime argued the painting likely shows the dawn of June 19, with the constellation Aries at its center. Two years later, astronomer Charles Whitney used weather records to rule out the 19th and suggested the sky was actually a composite. With the two theories at odds, the idea that Van Gogh painted real stars faded to what the authors call a “marginal minority view.” Most scholars granted only that the bright point near the horizon was likely Venus.

Kim’s team thought the stars were most likely real, and that the early studies were incomplete. No one had tested the painter’s field of view, the shape of its pattern of stars, and the historical record together. Plus, on the morning of June 19, the Moon was a waning gibbous, past half and nothing like the slim crescent depicted on the canvas.

Rather than assuming a date, the researchers asked whether the sky Van Gogh painted could occur, and when. Their method has two core steps. First, they compare a painted sky with astronomical simulations to see whether its arrangement is even possible. Then, they use a statistical method that measures the similarity between two shapes — called Procrustes analysis — to see how faithfully the painting’s star patterns matched real ones. When more historical data is available, the team also works to reconstruct the artist’s field of view from the landscape and check candidate dates against weather records and letters.

As a proof of concept, they applied the method to a handful of other night paintings. A 2011 study had argued that the stars in German painter Adam Elsheimer’s 1609 The Flight into Egypt weren’t copied from a single real sky, and the team’s test agreed. The painting’s Big Dipper sits at an angle to the Milky Way that never occurs in the night sky.

French painter Jean-François Millet’s Starry Night, painted between 1850 and 1865, has no letters, weather records, or known painting site to draw on, so the team ran only the core astronomical tests. The painting’s depiction of Orion — including the bright star Rigel — and Sirius revealed the tightest fit of any painting in the study to an actual star pattern.

Van Gogh’s own 1888 Starry Night Over the Rhône and Café Terrace at Night were next. Because their painting sites in Arles still stand and his letters and local weather records survive, they got the full treatment. The Big Dipper in Starry Night Over the Rhône matched the real sky with what the authors call “relative precision,” and the Summer Triangle in Café Terrace at Night fit even more closely.

RELATED: How Galileo blended science and art before his death 380 years ago

Then came The Starry Night. Van Gogh is thought to have based the painting’s village on an earlier sketch he had completed of Saint-Rémy. After locating a ridge near the asylum whose view matches the drawing, the team found the sketch captures a zoomed-in slice of the landscape. Measured diagonally, from one corner of the frame to the opposite corner, the field of view spans about 18°. They then made an assumption: that Van Gogh likely framed the painting’s night sky the same way. If he did, there should be a morning on record when the Moon, Venus, and a recognizable star pattern all fit inside that narrow frame and are arranged as painted.

Only three dawns in June and July 1889 put the Moon above Venus, as in the painting, inside that field of view: June 23, July 22, and July 23. Only the July mornings also placed the Hyades in view, and on July 22, the Moon and Venus sat in opposite corners with the cluster in between.

At the center of the canvas, a cluster of stars forms a lopsided V that closely resembles the shape of the Hyades cluster. But any handful of dots can coincidentally look like a V. The team had to evaluate whether these stars were intentionally painted as the Hyades or just happened to resemble the cluster’s shape.

They reasoned that if Van Gogh had painted the real cluster, each painted star should have a real counterpart in the same spot. That is, the star at the point of the painted V should match the star at the point of the real V, and so on down each arm of the cluster. Matching them up that way should give a better fit than any other possible combination. So the team tried every possible way of pairing the five painted stars with the five real ones — 120 combinations in all — and measured how well each fit. The natural, point-to-point pairing was the most accurate.

The match was close, but it wasn’t perfect. Of all the paintings the team tested, The Starry Night fit its stars with the lowest statistical accuracy. The authors also acknowledge that a close shape match alone can’t identify a star group. Some other constellations score as well or better and are ruled out only by other evidence, such as where they sat in the sky that morning. Nevertheless, the combined evidence still suggested July 22 as the most likely date.

One objection to this scenario remained. Historians have long held that a severe seizure in mid-July left Van Gogh unable to work. But after rereading his letters alongside weather records from that summer, the team argues the seizure may have struck later, in the last week of July, leaving Van Gogh free to watch the dawn sky on July 22.

The authors argue that the fact that so many clues converge on one morning makes it “hard to reconcile with the premise that the sky on the canvas is an arbitrary creation.” The simpler explanation is that the painting’s sky is rooted in actual observations of the sky on that morning in July — and the burden of proof now falls on anyone who says Van Gogh invented it.

Because the core test needs nothing but the star positions, the team argues it could be turned on other night paintings, including many no one has yet examined. “I hope this work prompts a renewed, multidisciplinary look at the sequence and working methods of the Saint-Rémy paintings, and at Van Gogh’s movements that summer,” Kim said in a statement. “Our conversation with Van Gogh’s other skies is still ongoing. We plan to tell those stories one by one.”

Stellarium, the free planetarium software the team used to cross-check its simulations, lets you simulate the night sky from any place and date. Enter Saint-Rémy-de-Provence, France, and set the clock to a couple hours before sunrise on July 22, 1889. If the sky looks too bright, turn off the atmosphere in the view settings (or press the keyboard shortcut, A).

Then search for the Hyades cluster. Brilliant Venus sits on one side, a slim crescent Moon slightly higher on the other, and between them the V of the Hyades. You can scroll through the hours to watch the scene wheel up from the horizon before dawn. Then step back a day to July 21, or ahead to July 23 and 24, and watch the Moon slide toward Venus. Or compare the view with June 19, the date Boime proposed, and judge for yourself which sky best fits the canvas.

Brooks Mendenhall is a staff writer at Astronomy, based in Chattanooga, Tennessee, fueled by an unending curiosity about the universe. A former classroom teacher, he has a knack for breaking down complex concepts for a wide audience.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto âEnthusiastâ but trust me I'm getting there.

Ripple has had one of its busiest stretches in years. It won a national trust bank charter, launched new partnerships and talked up XRP throughout Ripple Swell. Yet XRP is still trading near $1.27. Billy Trajcevski, Head of Strategic Partnerships at UpTrade, says the reason has more to do with Rippleâs structure than with the news itself.

Many holders worry that RLUSD, Rippleâs dollar stablecoin, is taking attention away from XRP. Trajcevski rejected that idea.

âXRP is not a stablecoin. RLUSD is. Itâs really as simple as that,â Trajcevski said.

Trajcevski argued that if XRP competed with RLUSD as a dollar substitute, it would simply sit at $1 forever, which no holder wants. âIf XRP replaces the dollar, weâre all screwed because the price is going to remain flat,â Trajcevski said.

According to Trajcevski, the two assets have different jobs:

A business paying an international invoice in another currency needs a float of XRP to do it, Trajcevski said, and RLUSD alone cannot do that job.

Asked why the good news has not shown up in XRPâs price, Trajcevski pointed to a basic business reality. Ripple is a private company that answers to its shareholders, and shareholders want to see several sources of revenue, not a business that depends on one asset.

âIf your entire business model is based on the success of the XRP token, the board will not be happy, shareholders will not be happy,â Trajcevski said.

That is the message many XRP holders do not want to hear. Ripple will keep building use cases and demand for XRP because that is in its interest, Trajcevski said, but it will not stake its whole business on the token.

Trajcevski also described how Rippleâs public stance toward XRP has changed. During the SEC lawsuit, Ripple kept its distance from the token, as any company in its position would. Now that the case is resolved, Trajcevski said, the company has âcome full circle,â and open support for XRP is back.

The clearest result from Swell, in Trajcevskiâs view, is that XRP has the full backing of the Ripple team.

Trajcevski picked out one headline from Swell that drew little attention: BNY, the worldâs largest custodian, now offers custody for XRP.

BNY serves major institutions, not retail investors. If it is offering XRP custody, Trajcevski said, there is clear institutional demand for the token, or Ripple has made it clear that demand is coming. Trajcevski called it the most bullish news of the event, even though it looked the least important.

Trajcevski also pointed to a session with Bullish CEO Tom Farley. Bullish already uses RLUSD in some of its products, and according to Trajcevski, the exchange is now in active talks with Ripple about new ways to use XRP that do not exist yet.

French Hill’s race still too close to call as House flips, changing the direction of crypto legislation

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Rep. Hill has led the House Financial Services Committee for the past two years, during which members passed the first federal framework for stablecoins as well as a sweeping crypto bill.

As of Wednesday afternoon, House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill's race for reelection is too close to call as a Democratic takeover in the House is set to usher in a new slate of leaders and reshape the future of crypto legislation in Congress.

On Tuesday night into Wednesday, Hill has been neck and neck with Democratic candidate Chris Jones in Arkansas, according to the Associated Press, with 99% of votes already reported. Hill held a slim lead at publication time.

Hill has led the House Financial Services Committee for the past two years, during which members passed the first federal framework for stablecoins as well as a sweeping crypto bill. The latter stalled in the Senate in September. Crypto political action committee Fairshake and other PACs spent $1 million on Hill's campaign, according to reporting from the Arkansas Democrat Gazette.

Other crypto-friendly lawmakers also secured reelection, including Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., Rep. Warren Davidson, R-Ohio, Rep. Ritchie Torres, D-N.Y., and current House Agriculture Committee Chair Glenn "GT" Thompson, R-Pa. Later in the day on Wednesday, Rep. Bill Huizenga, R-MI., who is viewed favorably by the crypto industry, lost his seat to Democratic candidate Sean McCann, according to the Associated Press.

Candidate Shomari Figures beat Republican candidate Rhett Marques in Alabama. Figures was endorsed by crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, given his vote on sweeping digital asset legislation called the Clarity Act.

With the House flipping control, Democrats will usher in the agenda for key congressional committees that have jurisdiction over crypto and prediction markets. Top Democrat Maxine Waters will likely take back her spot as House Financial Services Committee chair, leaving Hill out of his top spot, but likely to be the top Republican on that committee.

Waters is still expected to take up the spot again as chair, but has since been challenged by Democratic Reps. Sean Casten and Bill Foster. Some Democrats have signaled that they want "generational change," given that Waters is 88 years old and Casten is 54, according to reporting from Politico last month.

Regarding crypto, Waters worked on the stablecoin framework dubbed the GENIUS Act and has shown an openness to writing rules for the industry. However, she has also criticized the industry following the collapse of crypto exchange FTX and, later, President Donald Trump's crypto interests and how that can influence regulation.

Famed Iron Age king was actually a woman

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 4, 2026 7:01 PM EST

Ancient Rome famously invaded Britain in 43 CE, under the direction of the emperor Claudius. But his campaign’s success largely hinged on insider information. Just three years’ earlier, an Iron Age tribe living in present-day southeast England known as the Atrebates ousted their ruler named Verica, who subsequently fled for Rome. Once there, Verica convinced Claudius to move forward with plans to cross the English Channel, and add Britain to the empire’s ever-growing domain. In exchange for restoring his place on the Atrebates’ throne, Verica agreed to provide Roman troops with tactical support and guidance.

In a new discussion published today in the journal Current Archaeology, researchers argue that historians have consistently gotten one key detail incorrect for at least 200 years. Based on their analysis of ancient artifacts and contextual information, the Iron Age ruler Verica was a woman, not a man.

“Verica is assumed to have been male because of the patriarchal mindset of 19th century classical historians,” study co-author and Bournemouth University archaeologist Miles Russell said in a statement. “Verica was not only critical to the special relationship between Britain and Rome in the early first century [CE] but was indisputably female.”

The consequential re-evaluation is based on three major pieces of evidence—the first simply being Verica’s name. Ending in “a” was common for women’s names in Iron Age Britain. Two of Verica’s most recognizable contemporaries, Boudica of the Iceni and Cartimandua of the Brigantes, were both female leaders of their respective tribes. Boudica is particularly famous for leading a failed revolt against the Romans in 60 CE, but Russell and his colleagues argue their revision makes Verica a much more consequential figure.

The second reason relates to Roman accounts of Claudius. Many Roman writers of the era were highly critical of the emperor, and described him as weak or inept. Critics often cited his consultations with women and the formerly enslaved as proof. Despite his success in Britain, Roman historians like Cassius Dio tied his decision to Verica, a woman.

“The story of Claudius committing to something only because a woman told him to, fits the trope of this emperor as being submissive and henpecked,” argued Russell. “In effect, Dio was saying that the campaign was undertaken solely because of the whisperings of a barbarian femme fatale.”

Finally, the archaeologists point to Verica’s own coinage. Once reinstated to her throne, the ruler was allowed to mint her own currency featuring the Latin title “rex” to indicate she enjoyed the emperor’s full support. Many people mistakenly assume rex is only a masculine term, when it was actually applied to any gender.

“Both male and female kings were recognised by Rome, Cleopatra of Egypt being the most famous,” Russell added.

Verica’s image on the coins also reinforces the argument. Other British Iron Age leaders also produced coins, but those portray clearly male fighters. Verica’s currency shows a slender, feminine individual on horseback with bare breasts. Russell argues that taking all of this “at face value” shows Verica was clearly a woman—and a very powerful woman at that.

“Had she not been deposed, fleeing to Claudius for military support, Rome may never have invaded Britain in the first place,” he said.

Bitcoin price slips below $77K – Why post-election BTC crash fears persist

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

The voters have spoken. Now, the focus shifts to how investors react.

The chances of Democrats winning have already increased to 99%, and today’s outcome has seen them sweep both houses of Congress (Senate and House of Representatives). The question now is whether this shift brings some certainty to the market and allows Bitcoin to recover, or will it add another layer of volatility.

According to the latest Santiment data, Bitcoin retail sentiment has become increasingly bearish after the election results. The chart below shows that there are now only 0.51 bullish comments for every bearish comment. With many traders viewing the Republicans as more crypto-friendly, the Democratic sweep has added another layer of uncertainty to an already weak market structure.

Notably, this uncertainty is now showing up across both on-chain and technical indicators.

According to CryptoQuant, Bitcoin’s bounce from recent lows appears to be slowing, with low spot demand and prolonged liquidations taking Bitcoin towards $76,800. Put simply, there is some spot buying, but it isn’t strong enough to offset the selling and liquidation from the futures market. Meanwhile, BTC dominance has faced its third rejection near the 61% range, failing to break higher. This signals fading relative strength.

Together, Bitcoin [BTC] slipping below $77k, weak on-chain momentum, and increasingly bearish retail sentiment are creating a very fragile market structure as investors digest the Democratic sweep. Therefore, is the recent rally in the Bitcoin Fear and Greed Index simply a case of the bears taking a break before sentiment falls into “extreme” fear as the Bitcoin crash narrative takes hold?

Bitcoin’s Fear and Greed Index dropped to 26 for the first time in three months.

There is no doubt that the FUD surrounding elections affected sentiment, but since the election has occurred, the index has recovered over 30%. This shows that due to the Democratic sweep, there is less political uncertainty in the market, which has led to a short-term recovery in sentiment. However, on-chain and technical indicators have yet to show the same recovery.

According to AMBCrypto, this gap between sentiment and market momentum could be important. As noted by one analyst, Bitcoin has historically faced sell-offs after elections, raising the possibility of a 30% correction if the pattern repeats. For now, improving sentiment has kept Bitcoin from sliding into extreme fear; however, clear signs of accumulation are still missing.

This leaves a clear divergence between investor confidence and underlying market demand.

Unless this gap starts to narrow, the latest rebound in Bitcoin’s Fear and Greed Index may just be a short-term bounce after the elections. So, while the Democratic sweep may have taken away some of the political uncertainty, it has yet to change Bitcoin’s underlying market structure.

In this context, another midterm-driven sell-off cannot be ruled out.

Bitcoin’s weak demand, falling dominance, and bearish retail sentiment point to continued market pressure despite the recent sentiment recovery.

The Fear and Greed Index has bounced over 30%, but buying strength remains weak, leaving room for another Bitcoin sell-off.

This self-powered toothbrush makes electricity to kill cavity-causing bacteria

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Researchers have created a self-charging toothbrush that helps fight the bacteria linked to cavities and gum disease while preventing plaque buildup.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A toothbrush that generates its own electricity with every stroke could offer a new way to fight the microbes behind cavities and gum disease.

Conventional toothbrushes rely on physical scrubbing to remove plaque from teeth and gums. But even after a thorough brush, some bacteria remain and begin rebuilding plaque. To solve that problem, researchers have developed a self-powered toothbrush that converts the friction of brushing into an electrical charge. That electricity is intended to zap the plaque-promoting bacteria and keep them from colonizing teeth.

"It's great to have new approaches that actually enhance the effect of mechanical cleaning," said Nicholas Jakubovics, an oral microbiologist at Newcastle University in the U.K. who was not involved in the study. "The idea that the toothbrush surface itself is actively doing something is really interesting."

The experimental brush has bristles made from polyvinylidene fluoride (PVDF), a plastic that generates electricity when bent; these types of energy-producing materials are called piezoelectric. As the bristles flex during brushing, they produce an electrical charge that then triggers chemical reactions in saliva and water. These reactions release reactive oxygen species (ROS), which are highly reactive molecules that can damage bacterial cells.

Even when the bristles are not being used to brush, they carry a faint surface charge that helps prevent bacteria from clinging to the brush head when the toothbrush isn't in use, the team reported Wednesday (Nov. 4) in the journal Science Advances.

In laboratory tests where the toothbrush was used on isolated germs, its electrified bristles killed more than half of the Streptococcus mutans bacteria present. These microbes contribute to tooth decay. By comparison, nylon bristles killed just 9% of the bacteria. They also reduced levels of Porphyromonas gingivalis, a bacterium linked to gum disease, by up to 78%, while nylon brushes reduced them by less than 8%.

When the researchers added an antioxidant — a compound that neutralizes ROS — the new toothbrush's antibacterial effect largely disappeared, highlighting that the ROS are likely the key to its effects.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

The team also found that the activity of certain genes in both S. mutans and P. gingivalis fell after exposure to the electrified bristles. These genes are involved in making ATP-binding cassette (ABC) transporters — molecular pumps that help shuttle essential nutrients across bacterial cell membranes. The researchers suspect that disrupting this machinery makes it harder for these microbes to survive and cling to teeth.

The new toothbrush still needs to be tested in further safety trials.

But Jakubovics was not convinced by that part of the team's explanation. "ABC transporters transport all sorts of different things," he said, adding that the findings did not clearly show how changes in these genes affected the bacteria.

The researchers also tested the new brush against plaque biofilms — hardy communities of bacteria that stick together and cling to tooth surfaces. For the experiment, they grew human dental plaque on cow tooth enamel and found that the brush removed more than 99% of the biofilm, outperforming a regular nylon brush.

Finally, the team moved to humans, putting the brush to the test in 115 adults with gum inflammation or tooth decay. These study participants were randomly assigned to use either the new toothbrushes or traditional nylon-bristled brushes twice a day for three months. (The study didn't note whether participants noticed a difference in sensation when using the electrified brushes.)

By the end of the trial, the participants who used the electrified bristles had less plaque, less gum inflammation and less bleeding than the participants who used ordinary brushes. Plaque samples also showed that the people who used electrified brushes had lower levels of bacteria associated with oral disease and higher levels of beneficial bacteria, which have previously been reported to suppress harmful oral bacteria and support oral health.

The researchers think that the disease-linked bacteria may have relatively fragile cell membranes, making them more vulnerable to damage from ROS than the beneficial bacteria. That's a theory that requires further study, though, so it's not completely clear how selective the brush is against plaque-causing bacteria.

In a test of the toothbrush's durability, the PVDF bristles retained their structure and electrical properties over three months of simulated use. Lab-grown mouse cells repeatedly exposed to brush material showed no signs of damage, and brushing on cow tooth enamel did not increase surface roughness. However, these tests cannot establish whether repeated use of these brushes is safe for humans.

"We would want to make sure that it is safe on the tissues for long-term use," Jakubovics said. He noted that, in lab tests, bacteria are grown directly on the bristles and exposed to their electricity for hours, "whereas in reality, in the mouth, your bristle is only in contact with the tooth or oral tissue for a very short time."

The trial with humans provides some insight into how the brush works in real life, but it was relatively small. Larger studies will be needed to find out whether the toothbrush offers a meaningful advantage over conventional brushes. For now, Jakubovics sees the recent study as just a proof of concept; more information and more convincing clinical data are still needed, he emphasized.

This article is for informational purposes only and is not meant to offer medical advice.

Lu, Y., Song, J., Chi, X., Li, W., Wu, L., Guo, Y., Cui, Q., Zheng, X., Bai, Y., Heng, B. C., Han, X., Zhang, X., & Deng, X. (2026). Self-charged toothbrush facilitates tooth healthcare maintenance. Science Advances, 12, Article eaeg5744. https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aeg5744

Isha Ishtiaq is a freelance health journalist based in Pakistan who covers health, medicine and biomedical research. Her work has appeared in Live Science, Science News, and Medscape, among other outlets. She holds a B.S. (Hons) in biotechnology from the University of Gujrat and an M.S. in biological sciences from the University of Sialkot, where her research focused on the use of deep learning models for disease detection.

Anthropic Strikes Its First Licensing Deal, With the AP, for Election Coverage

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

This story was originally published in On Background with Mark Stenberg, a free, weekly newsletter that explores the key themes shaping the media industry. You can sign up for it here.

If, by chance, you opened Claude on Election Night and asked it for an update on the latest polling data, you would have been treated to a novel sight: up-the-minute information, provided by and attributed to the Associated Press.

This new development was the result of a content and data licensing deal struck between the two organizations, which was struck just before the midterm elections, according to two people familiar with the matter.

Anthropic did not respond to a request for comment, and the AP did not provide a comment on the record. The financial specifics of the arrangement, as well as other critical details, such as its timeframe, were not disclosed by either party.

Nonetheless, the deal marks a dramatic evolution in strategy for Anthropic, as the artificial intelligence firm behind Claude had not, until Tuesday, struck a content licensing deal with any digital publisher.

In that regard, Claude differed dramatically from its primary competitor, ChatGPT, whose parent company OpenAI has negotiated more than a dozen content licensing deals with major publishers, including Axel Springer, News Corp., and The Atlantic, among others.

In fact, the reluctance by Anthropic to engage in any kind of content licensing discussion had become such a subject of intrigue within the industry that I explored the situation in August, in one of my more literally titled pieces.

At the time, digital media executives told me that the company genuinely believes that its scraping and use of publisher content is legally permissible under the concept of fair use. As such, it had not moved to enter into any such partnerships, nor had it indicated any plans to do so in the future.

So why change its tactics now, especially as the company prepares to go public in a matter of weeks?

FOR Scoops, Sharp Analysis, and Insider Context

The likely answer is that Anthropic finally admitted what publishers have long known to be true, which is that an information index without access to up-to-date data is dramatically limited in its utility.

Because Anthropic refused to strike content licensing deals with major news publishers, it lacked access to the kind of credible, timely reporting that consumers turn to in key moments like an election cycle.

Without the ability to intake data from premium publishers, whose websites block its crawlers, Anthropic could only cite what AI researcher Deana Burke calls the “unlocked web.” These low-credibility sites might serve as sufficient sources of information for low-stakes queries, but they cannot tell a user which politician won a Senate seat 10 minutes ago.

As such, Anthropic was facing down the prospect of its users finding its platform to be nearly useless as a source of real-time information on one of the most important days of the year for search queries.

Publishers had been blocking Anthropic, as well as other answer engines, to produce this very outcome. The tactic is effectively a digital embargo, with news outlets withholding their content in hopes of bringing answer engines to the bargaining table to strike licensing deals. And in this limited instance, the strategy worked.

Of course, the AP is a bit of an exceptional case: It generates the vast majority of its revenue via licensing and wire deals with other publishers, so much of its business is structured around striking these kinds of tie-ups already. The AP was also the first publisher with whom Google and OpenAI signed licensing agreements.

The AP also has the advantage of being a canonically non-partisan outlet—it positions itself as more of a service, like Getty Images for information, than a standard publisher, which often has an editorial slant one way or the other. For these reasons, the AP makes for an ideal partner for an AI firm looking to test out licensing opportunities.

This limited foray into licensing is by no means an indicator that Anthropic has irrevocably changed its licensing strategy. In all likelihood, the company will use the data from this experiment to determine whether it wants to go further down this path or not.

But the deal does make a compelling case for the value of credible, real-time information to answer engines. And if Anthropic wants to stake its claim as the dominant platform in the ecosystem, it is hard to imagine how it does so without premium publishers.

Closing on OpenWeb (SCOOP): The venture firm Savage Ventures is preparing a bid of between $60 million to $70 million to acquire OpenWeb, the bankrupt Israeli adtech firm once worth $1.5 billion, according to an inside source. A distressed sale would be the latest grim chapter in the long, sordid saga of OpenWeb, whose fortunes have fallen precipitously in recent years. The startup, whose 2021 Series E received investment from The New York Times, helps publishers monetize their comments sections, a commercial model whose appeal is dwindling as the open web continues to contract. Still, its data, publisher relationships, and on-page real estate make it a reasonable acquisition target at just 4% of its high watermark valuation.

Creator Agency Consolidation (EXCLUSIVE): On Thursday, the social-first agency LV8 merged with its sister company Mirage Digital. Following the merger, the newly combined firm raised a round of equity funding “in the high eight figures” from Velocity Capital, according to LV8 chief executive Griffin Haddrill. The deal is the latest in a series of creator and social-centric tie-ups in the agency space in recent weeks. In September alone, the social agency SAMY acquired the influencer shop Get Engaged, and Billion Dollar Boy bought the business-to-business influencer marketing agency Creator Authority. In the first half of 2026, there were 70 creator economy M&A transactions, up 23% year-over-year, according to Quartermast Advisors, and agencies have accounted for around a fifth of those. Expect more from me on this front in the next week or two!

People Inc. People: On Thursday, People Inc. laid off around 6% of its staff, a total of nearly 200 employees, according to an internal memo I obtained. The cuts primarily affected its print and “legacy digital” businesses, the latest in a growing constellation of data points that reflect the diminishing commercial prospects of the open web. Of course, People Inc. is not alone in its reductions: Netflix, Disney, and Skydance, among others, have all enacted layoffs ahead of the holiday season.

On Thursday, at the Creator Marketing Summit in New York, I spoke on-stage with Tyler Stewart, the head of marketing and creative partnerships at Gopuff. Before the panel, I asked Tyler about the news that had broken earlier that morning, that two of his competitors—Instacart and Doordash—planned to combine in a $17 billion merger.

He offered, understandably, a rehearsed response: that Gopuff is in a category of its own because it owns its inventory and delivers from its own locations, unlike the middleman services its peers provide. That point of differentiation is a cornerstone of the Gopuff business; but another, more salient difference is its heavy use of creator marketing and collaboration.

In June, Gopuff launched a coconut water brand with Tom Brady, and last month Druski took an equity stake in the company as part of a partnership. The tactics stem largely from fiscal responsibility—Tyler said several times that budgetary constraints were a factor in his decision-making—but it also positions the company to capitalize on the growing momentum behind the creator space.

Mark Stenberg: Creators are a cornerstone of Gopuff’s marketing. How did that come about, and how has the strategy evolved?

Tyler Stewart: We’re still a privately funded startup, and we do not have the budget, frankly, to spend crazy TV dollars. Back in the day, our approach was to scale, spray, and pray, but we’ve evolved. Now, we want to go deeper and longer with a core crew of partners who can help us punch above our weight and reach audiences we can’t reach ourselves.

Mark: You also build products with creators. How does that work?

Tyler: Because we’re first party, we have a full private-label business and manufacturing capabilities that we can bring to our partners, which allows us to have a little fun with it. But it’s also more complex. A lot of times, creators don’t know what they want to do or how to do it, so we need to be that partner for them.

Mark: What’s the best example of what this model can unlock?

Tyler: Two years ago for Halloween, we made a massive chocolate bar with Alix Earle. We delivered it to her house in L.A. for her party and generated content around it. Then we made a regular-size version for our whole network, and it was gone in three minutes.

Mark: How do you decide which creators to work with?

Tyler: There are massive celebrities we’ve onboarded thinking, “They have hundreds of millions of followers, they’re going to sell the shit out of this.” And then they don’t. So, can they get us eyeballs we wouldn’t get otherwise? Do they have a level of authenticity, where they actually give a shit about the product? What’s proven not to work for us is the transactional version.

Mark: Druski’s deal with Gopuff included equity. How do you think about ownership for creators?

Tyler: We don’t have cash, so we need somebody who believes in us and wants to ride this wave with us. Druski is hilarious, and we’re going to let him do what he does best. But he has to accept that he’s not getting the check that PrizePicks or T-Mobile would pay him for a commercial.

Mark: You can’t give equity to every creator. What does it take to earn it?

Tyler: Can you help us grow our brand and business? Not everybody can. We put a lot of performance deals in place: Help us get a certain number of customers, and I’ll give you more equity. Why would the business want you as an owner if you’re not helping the business?

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/cms-finalizes-final-medicare-rule-deeper-cuts-physician-pay-delays-rpm-restrictions-2029" hreflang="en">CMS finalizes steeper Medicare physician pay cuts for 2027, delays restriction on third-party RPM vendors</a>

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →
CMS finalizes steeper Medicare physician pay cuts for 2027, delays restriction on third-party RPM vendors" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services finalized sweeping Medicare payment reforms it says will shift the program from volume-based "sick care" toward prevention, primary care and accountable care.

The 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule rule includes a roughly 2% reduction in baseline physician payment rates with targeted increases and new payment opportunities for accountable care, primary and chronic care and behavioral health. CMS finalized steeper physician payment cuts than it had proposed in July.

An unpublished PDF version of the 2,468-page final rule can be found here.

In a press release, CMS said it is "prioritizing prevention, strengthening primary care, cracking down on duplicate billing and reinforcing fiscal stewardship across Medicare."

The final rule modernizes physician payment structures to "reward health maintenance and coordinated primary care rather than disjointed, volume-based intervention," CMS said.

"Medicare should reward clinicians for keeping people healthy," HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr. said in a statement. "Under President Trump’s leadership, we are putting prevention and primary care at the center of Medicare, closing payment loopholes, and cracking down on waste. This rule directs resources toward coordinated care that puts patients first. We are changing what Medicare pays for to change what our health system delivers."

Under the final rule, CMS will officially sunset the traditional Merit-based Incentive Payment System (MIPS) reporting option beginning with the CY 2031 performance period. Clinicians will have through CY 2030 to transition to the MIPS Value Pathways (MVPs), which already cover approximately 98% of clinicians, or Advanced Alternative Payment Models. This reflects two additional years of transition time compared to the proposed rule. The rule also finalizes three new MVPs focused on diabetes, hypertension, and hospitalists, and requires clinicians to report at least one specialty-specific "Core Measure" starting in CY 2027.

For physicians, while the proposed rule includes statutory payment increases, Medicare payment rates face an overall baseline decrease for 2027 due to the expiration of a temporary 2.5% legislative increase from the One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) in 2026.

Under the Medicare Access and CHIP Reauthorization Act (MACRA), the CY2027 conversion factors reflect statutory updates of +0.75% for qualifying Alternative Payment Model (APM) participants and +0.25% for non-qualifying participants. The changes to the PFS conversion factors for CY 2027 include these updates as required by statute and an estimated -0.12% adjustment necessary to account for proposed changes in work RVUs for some services, according to CMS in a fact sheet.

For clinicians who don't qualify through an advanced alternative payment model, the conversion factor drops from $33.40 to $32.63, a 2.31% reduction. For qualifying APM participants, the conversion factor drops 1.83% from $33.57 to $32.95. In the draft rule issued in July, CMS projected cuts of 1.68% and 1.19%, respectively.

CMS is also addressing what it sees as overpayment and duplicate reimbursement when multiple services are delivered during the same patient encounter. Under the final rule, when an office visit and minor procedure occur on the same day, CMS will reduce payment by 20% for the lower-cost service, reducing duplication in payment by patients and the Medicare program for clinical work already covered in the procedure’s valuation.

CMS had initially proposed paying the higher-cost service in full and reducing payment for the other service by 50%.

Physician groups were quick to criticize CMS' payment changes in the final rule.

"Notwithstanding modest policy improvements to enhance value-based care through MSSP, MGMA is disappointed by CMS’s decision to finalize several destabilizing policies in the 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule," Anders Gilberg, senior vice president of government affairs at the Medical Group Management Association, said in a statement.

"Conversion factor cuts of 1.83% for qualifying APM participants and 2.31% for all other clinicians further degrade an already fragile payment system and amplify severe financial challenges caused by years of inadequate Medicare reimbursement," Gilberg said.

Compounding this ongoing reimbursement decline, CMS will cut payments by 20% when office visits billed with modifier 25 are performed on the same day as certain procedures, "interfering with physician practices’ ability to offer same-day care," he noted.

MGMA is urging Congress to intervene by immediately fixing cuts to the conversion factor, along with changes to modifier 25. "To prevent annual disruption, Congress should prioritize comprehensive MACRA reform by passing the Patients First Act," Gilberg said.

The agency is overhauling the office/outpatient evaluation and management visit complexity add-on code, HCPCS code G2211. CMS is transitioning HCPCS code G2211 from a flat add-on payment to a modifier that would increase the payment of the associated E/M code by 16%. That would maintain an equal percentage increase across all levels of E/M codes, CMS said.

The agency also finalized a new modifier that would offset the costs incurred by practitioners in accountable care organizations (ACOs) when providing longitudinal care and care coordination. The modifier would only be available for practitioners participating in a Shared Savings Program ACO or participant providers in a Long-term Enhanced ACO Design (LEAD) Model ACO and would increase payment of the associated E/M visit by 32%, CMS said.

The final 2027 PFS rule also establishes new 340B reporting requirements. Covered entities will have to report certain Medicare Part D claims involving drugs purchased through the 340B Drug Pricing Program to the Medicare Part D Claims Data 340B Repository beginning with claims with a date of service on or after Jan.1, 2027. The deadline for the first quarterly submission is delayed until Sept. 30, 2027.

In the final rule, CMS tightened remote patient monitoring and remote therapeutic monitoring requirements but backed off an immediate restriction on contractors.

Beginning in 2027, as CMS outlined in the proposed rule, RTM services can only be furnished to established patients, and providers must provide a separately reportable initiating visit before billing for RPM or RTM services. CMS initially proposed only allowing payment for RPM or RTM services when performed by clinical staff employed by the practice and not when those services are delivered by third-party vendors. The agency provided a two-year reprieve on that requirement as it goes into effect Jan. 1, 2029, "giving practices that rely on contractors two additional years to adapt," CMS said.

Health tech companies, providers and lawmakers pushed back on the proposal to restrict third-party vendors, urging CMS to reconsider the proposed changes to preserve patients' access to these tech-enabled services. Many rural, small, and independent practices, as well as both urban and rural health systems, rely on specialized clinical and technology partners to make remote monitoring services available to their patients.

CMS finalized changes to the Medicare Shared Savings Program, the nation’s largest value-based payment program, to "accelerate accountable care service delivery, further CMS’ goal of aligning spending and value in Original Medicare, and support achieving other related strategic objectives," the agency said in a fact sheet.

The overhaul to the MSSP program aims to make ACO participation more financially attractive, encourage organizations to take on risk, expand beneficiary participation and ease some quality-reporting requirements.

The agency says the changes are projected to grow the program by adding up to 1 million assigned beneficiaries in an average year and produce $5.5 billion in net federal savings over 10 years. CMS also projects the changes will generate an overall increase in shared savings net of losses to ACOs by $1.1 billion over a decade.

The final rule changes the Shared Savings Program’s benchmarking and financial methodology to balance incentives between Level E of the BASIC track and the ENHANCED track. The shared savings rate for Level E of the BASIC track increases 50% to 60% and reduce the maximum weight used in calculating the positive regional adjustment for lower-spending ACOs participating under the ENHANCED track from 50% to 35%.

The agency is increasing the prior savings adjustment's scaling factor from 50% to 75%, allowing more of an ACO's previous savings to be reflected in future benchmarks.

Under the final rule, CMS will risk-adjust the 5% cap on benchmark increases, potentially allowing ACOs that care for sicker, higher-cost patients to receive larger upward adjustments.

Data shows that patients aligned with an ACO are less likely to experience uncontrolled diabetes or hypertension and are more likely to receive recommended preventive care, according to CMS. In performance year 2024, 75% of the 476 participating ACOs earned shared savings totaling $4.1 billion, while generating net savings for the Medicare Trust Funds of approximately $2.5 billion compared to projected spending benchmarks.

Under the final 2027 PFS rule, approved ACOs may reduce or eliminate Part B cost-sharing for many items and services for assigned beneficiaries, directly lowering out-of-pocket costs for essential care. That change goes into effect April 1, 2027.

The rule also increases shared savings rates for eligible ACOs, provides new incentives for first-time participants and sets more predictable spending targets. And, the rule simplifies electronic health record reporting requirements, streamlines patient notice mandates and removes outdated administrative hurdles.

I revealed yesterday that the new Xbox Nano controller supports a Wi-Fi connection directly to Xbox Cloud Gaming, and now Microsoft is making it official today. Two new Xbox previews will let players try out using a phone as a controller on smart TVs, as well as cloud connect on the Xbox Nano to connect directly to cloud servers.

“Instead of a Bluetooth connection to your TV, cloud connect uses your Wi-Fi to connect directly to the cloud server running your game,” says the Xbox team. “That can help reduce input latency, so gameplay may feel more responsive.”

Players in the US will be able to preview the new cloud connect option for the Xbox Nano controller starting today. If you’re not in the US you will be able to preview a new phone controller option on smart TVs. The controller is available on the Xbox Cloud Gaming website, allowing people to use the streaming service without a dedicated Bluetooth controller.

Microsoft says “both experiences are still being tested” and that “availability and supported games or devices may be limited” during this beta phase. You’ll need to use the Xbox beta app for Android or the Xbox Accessories app on PC to set up cloud connect on the Nano controller, and the smart TV phone controller just requires a toggle of preview features from the Settings > Account section of Xbox Cloud Gaming.

Aeroplan Stopovers — Two Cities for the Price of One

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Savings rate, FI number, and the path to freedom.

Withdrawal plans, Roth ladders, and early access.

Index funds, asset allocation, and real estate.

Roth conversions, capital gains, and FI tax planning.

Career growth, side hustles, and entrepreneurship.

Beliefs, purpose, and financial psychology.

Exercise, nutrition, and mental wellness.

Compare the best travel credit cards for maximizing points and miles.

New to travel rewards? Start here with our beginner-friendly guide.

Learn the fundamentals of earning and redeeming travel points.

Maximize free hotel stays with the right loyalty programs.

Fly for nearly free with smart airline mile strategies.

Build the perfect credit card portfolio for your spending habits.

Browse our complete library of travel rewards articles.

ChooseFI has partnered with CardRatings for our coverage of credit card products. ChooseFI and CardRatings may earn compensation from card issuers when a customer clicks on a link, when an application is approved, or when an account is opened. Opinions, reviews, analyses & recommendations are the author's alone, and have not been reviewed, endorsed or approved by any of these entities. American Express is a ChooseFI advertiser.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

One of the highest-value redemptions in travel rewards

Picture this: you're settling into a lie-flat business class seat somewhere over the Atlantic, a glass of something cold in hand, knowing that in 48 hours you'll be waking up in Istanbul — and then, a few days later, continuing onward to Bangkok. Two bucket-list cities. One award booking. One points balance. This is what the Aeroplan stopover sweet spot feels like in practice. It's not a trick or a loophole — it's a deliberately designed feature of Air Canada's Aeroplan program that the vast majority of travelers simply don't know exists. And that gap between awareness and reality is exactly where the ChooseFI community finds its edge. Here's the math that should make your jaw drop. A business class itinerary from North America to Istanbul with a continuing flight onward to Bangkok — two long-haul segments, lie-flat seats, Star Alliance lounges at every connection — would cost somewhere between **$6,000 and $15,000 in cash**. That's a realistic retail range depending on season and how far out you book. Most people never consider it a possibility for a family vacation or a milestone trip because the sticker price puts it in a different universe. With Aeroplan, you're booking that same itinerary — the same seats, the same lounges, the same Turkish Airlines or Thai Airways product — for **75,000 Aeroplan points**. At the low end of the cash price range, that's already **8 cents per point**. At the high end, you're extracting **20 cents per point** from a currency that most people earned by putting groceries on a credit card. The pricing inefficiency here is structural. Aeroplan's partner award chart prices long-haul business class at a fixed rate regardless of cash fare fluctuations. When business class prices spike — around holidays, during peak travel seasons, during major events — your points cost stays exactly the same. You're insulated from the market. That's the FI mindset applied to travel: you've already paid for the trip in points, slowly accumulated through everyday spending, and now you're simply redeeming the stored value. The stopover itself is nearly free. Aeroplan charges just 5,000 extra miles to add a stopover city to a one-way award. That means your Istanbul layover becomes a multi-day stay — at zero additional points cost beyond that small add-on — and you haven't booked a second award at all. One booking. Two destinations. That's the entire premise, and once you see it, you can't unsee it.

The mechanism behind this sweet spot is elegantly simple once you understand it, but it requires knowing three things that most travelers don't: how Aeroplan's routing rules work, what a stopover actually is in the context of an award ticket, and why Star Alliance's global network makes this scalable to almost any destination pair you can imagine.

When Aeroplan relaunched its loyalty program in 2020, it did something remarkable: it eliminated fuel surcharges entirely and published a partner award chart with fixed pricing for flights on Star Alliance carriers. This is critical. When you book Turkish Airlines business class through Aeroplan, you pay the Aeroplan fixed chart rate — not what Turkish Airlines would charge in its own currency or miles program, and not the inflated cash price. That fixed chart is the engine of this redemption.

For long-haul international business class, Aeroplan's chart prices routing through multiple regions based on your origin and destination — not every intermediate stop. This is the aha moment. Aeroplan determines your award price based on where your journey starts and where it ends, and then allows you to add a stopover in between for just 5,000 additional miles. The stopover city is priced as a pass-through for the purpose of calculating your award zone, not as a second trip.

On a one-way Aeroplan award, you're permitted to add a free stopover — meaning you can stay in an intermediate city for any length of time before continuing to your final destination. That 5,000-mile add-on is essentially the cost of a full additional destination. Compare that to booking a second one-way award from Istanbul to Bangkok, which would cost you another 40,000–55,000 Aeroplan points in business class. The math is decisive.

The routing must be geographically sensible — you can't fly backwards across the globe — but Aeroplan's allowance for multi-segment itineraries through Star Alliance hubs gives you enormous flexibility. Istanbul (IST) is a Turkish Airlines hub. Bangkok (BKK) is served by Thai Airways and several Star Alliance carriers. Both are Star Alliance partners. Both are bookable through Aeroplan at fixed chart rates with no fuel surcharges.

The best part for the ChooseFI community is how accessible the underlying points are. Aeroplan accepts transfers from four major programs:

Chase's instant transfer speed is particularly valuable here. When you find business class availability — which can disappear quickly — you want to move points immediately. Having Chase Ultimate Rewards as your primary earning currency means you can search, find space, and transfer points before the availability window closes. The 1:1 ratio across all four programs means no conversion math — every point you earn translates directly to one Aeroplan mile.

Booking an Aeroplan stopover award in business class is genuinely one of the more complex redemptions in the travel rewards world, but it becomes manageable when you break it into distinct phases. Here's exactly how to do it.

Start by deciding on your two destinations. Aeroplan stopovers work best when your intermediate city is a major Star Alliance hub that lies geographically between your origin and final destination. Classic combinations include North America to Istanbul (Turkish Airlines hub) continuing to Southeast Asia, or North America to a European hub continuing to the Middle East or East Africa. Your stopover city should be somewhere you genuinely want to spend 2–5 days — this is a real trip, not just a layover.

This is the most important and most time-consuming step. You need to find saver-level business class availability on both segments independently before you book.

Once you've confirmed availability on both segments, go to aeroplan.com and use the multi-city search function. Enter your full itinerary — origin, stopover city with your intended layover dates, and final destination.

Once you've confirmed the award is bookable and are ready to complete the reservation:

Complete your booking on aeroplan.com or with a phone agent, confirm the stopover is properly reflected in your itinerary, and save every confirmation email. Verify your seats on each carrier's website immediately after booking.

A stopover is an intentional break in your journey lasting more than 24 hours at a connecting city. Most airlines treat stopovers as separate tickets, charging full price for each segment. Aeroplan is one of the few programs that allows a free stopover on round-trip awards, letting you explore a connecting city for days before continuing to your final destination. This is fundamentally different from a layover, which is a brief connection of a few hours at an airport. The stopover must be in a city served by a Star Alliance carrier on your routing, and the key is that Aeroplan prices the entire itinerary as one award, not two.

Card links may earn ChooseFI a commission. See our advertiser disclosure.

Let's build a real example so you can see exactly what this looks like on paper — and in the air.

The Route: Toronto (YYZ) → Istanbul (IST, 4-day stopover) → Bangkok (BKK) The Carrier: Turkish Airlines business class on both segments The Points Cost: 75,000 Aeroplan miles for the two segments + 5,000 miles for the stopover = 80,000 Aeroplan miles total Cash Equivalent: A comparable business class itinerary on this routing typically retails for $9,000–$12,000 in 2026, depending on when you search and how far in advance you book.

The CPP Calculation: At the midpoint cash value of $10,500 against 80,000 points, you're extracting 13.1 cents per Aeroplan mile — well inside the 8–20 cent range this sweet spot delivers, and roughly 10–13 times the value of redeeming Aeroplan miles for cash back.

The Turkish Airlines business class product on long-haul routes is consistently rated among the top business class experiences in the Star Alliance network. On the YYZ–IST segment — operated on a 787 or A330 — you'll have access to a lie-flat seat with direct aisle access, the Turkish Airlines Lounge in Istanbul (widely considered one of the best airport lounges in the world, full stop), and a meal service that reflects the airline's culinary reputation. The IST lounge alone — with its restaurant, spa, golf simulator, and sleeping areas — is worth lingering in even before your onward connection.

In Istanbul, your four days might include the Hagia Sophia at sunrise, a Bosphorus dinner cruise, and the Grand Bazaar before you head to the airport to continue your journey. You've paid nothing additional for this stay beyond the 5,000-mile stopover fee.

The IST–BKK segment puts you into Bangkok in the evening, ready for Thailand's extraordinary food, culture, and cost of living — which, for the FI-minded traveler, means your lodging and meals on the ground are remarkably affordable, stretching the value of your trip even further.

Total points cost for two lie-flat business class segments and a full European city stay: 80,000 miles, earned on everyday credit card spending. That's the entire proposition, and those numbers are real.

Book at exactly 355 days out. Aeroplan opens its booking window at 355 days before departure. The best business class availability on popular routes — especially Turkish Airlines long-haul — appears at this window and gets claimed quickly. Set a calendar reminder and be ready to search at the 355-day mark for your target travel dates.

Use Chase Ultimate Rewards as your primary earning currency for this redemption. The instant transfer speed to Aeroplan is genuinely differentiating. When you find award space and need to move quickly, Chase lets you transfer and book in the same session. Amex, Capital One, and Bilt all require 1–2 days, during which your availability can evaporate.

Search availability before you transfer points. Confirm space exists on every segment before a single point moves. There's no undo button on a transfer, and Aeroplan points sitting idle in your account are fine — points transferred to an account where you can't find availability are a headache.

Call Aeroplan for complex routings. The online multi-city tool is functional but can struggle with nuanced stopover configurations. Phone agents are experienced with these bookings and have the same access to inventory. The call is worth it for an $80,000-point booking.

Leverage the open-jaw rule too. Aeroplan also allows open-jaw routing — fly into one city, depart from another. Combined with the stopover, you can design a route that visits three geographic points on a single award: fly into Istanbul, take trains or ferries to Athens, and fly home from Athens. The award prices the same way.

Don't assume partner availability matches Aeroplan's search. Turkish Airlines and other Star Alliance partners don't always make every seat available through Aeroplan's online portal. If you see space on the carrier's own site but not on aeroplan.com, call — agents can often access inventory the website misses.

Don't book inflexible connecting flights. Long-haul itineraries with tight connections are a risk. Build in at least a 3-hour connection at hub airports to protect against irregular operations that could affect your award segments.

Don't earn Marriott Bonvoy points with this redemption in mind. The 3:1 transfer ratio to Aeroplan makes Marriott a poor choice for funding this specific award. Stick to Amex, Chase, Capital One, or Bilt at 1:1.

Don't book this for a time-sensitive trip. Award availability, transfer timelines, and multi-carrier coordination mean this requires planning runway. Build in at least 3–4 weeks of lead time beyond the actual booking date.

This redemption is purpose-built for the advanced travel rewards enthusiast in the ChooseFI community — someone who has already mastered the basics (earning transferable points, understanding award charts, booking straightforward partner awards) and is ready to unlock the next level of value.

The ideal profile: A couple or solo traveler planning a milestone trip — a significant anniversary, a sabbatical, a once-in-a-decade adventure — who wants to experience two destinations in business class without paying business class prices for either one. You're flexible on travel dates, comfortable spending time on the phone with an airline agent, and genuinely excited about the trip-planning process as part of the experience.

Minimum points balance: You'll need approximately 80,000 Aeroplan miles to complete this booking (75,000 for the award plus 5,000 for the stopover). That's achievable from a single premium credit card welcome bonus, a combination of two or three mid-tier welcome bonuses, or 12–18 months of everyday spending on a 2x transferable points card.

For the ChooseFI traveler who's been building transferable points for a year or two and wants to see what this system is truly capable of, this redemption is the answer.

This redemption is rated **Hard** — and that rating is honest. It earns that designation from three compounding challenges, not just one. **Challenge 1: Finding availability on multiple segments simultaneously.** Business class award space on popular long-haul routes is limited. Finding saver-level availability on one segment is manageable. Finding it on two connected segments, with dates that create a coherent stopover itinerary, requires persistence and flexibility. This is the primary barrier. **Challenge 2: Understanding Aeroplan's routing logic.** Aeroplan's stopover rules, multi-city booking interface, and the distinction between how partner awards are priced require more than surface-level familiarity with the program. A misunderstanding of how the stopover is added can result in a significantly higher points cost or a booking that doesn't reflect your intended itinerary. **Challenge 3: Coordinating the transfer timing.** With a 355-day booking window and fast-moving business class availability, the timing of your point transfers requires active management — particularly if you're not using Chase's instant transfer speed. ## Three Tips to Overcome These Challenges 1. **Start your availability research 60–90 days before the 355-day window opens.** Know your target route, your preferred stopover city, and your date range before you ever log into aeroplan.com. Preparation turns a chaotic search into a focused one. 2. **Use united.com as your availability research tool.** It searches the Star Alliance network and shows you realistic inventory without requiring you to commit points. 3. **Don't be discouraged by the learning curve.** Every hard redemption in the ChooseFI community gets easier the second time. Plenty of members who now book these routinely considered them impossible the first time they looked. The complexity is real — but so is the $10,000 of value sitting on the other side of it.

Pick a final destination and a stopover city on the same Star Alliance routing. Classic combos: Istanbul + Bangkok, Zurich + Tokyo, Vienna + Singapore. The stopover city must be a natural connection point on a Star Alliance route between your origin and destination.

Set a calendar alert for 355 days before your departure date. Search for business class availability on each segment separately using United.com or Aeroplan.com. Both legs need saver-level award space on the same booking.

Before transferring any points, verify that saver award space exists on both the outbound leg (origin to stopover city) and the continuation (stopover city to final destination). If either segment is unavailable, the booking will not work.

Transfer Chase Ultimate Rewards (instant) or Amex Membership Rewards (1-2 days) to your Aeroplan account at a 1:1 ratio. Only transfer once you have confirmed availability on both segments.

Call Aeroplan or use the multi-city search tool online. Specify your stopover city and duration (up to the maximum allowed). The system prices it as one round-trip award. Confirm total points match expectations before completing the booking.

Aeroplan allows stopovers of up to 45 days on round-trip awards. You can stay in your stopover city for a few days or several weeks, giving you genuine time to explore rather than just passing through.

No, stopovers are only available on round-trip awards through Aeroplan. One-way bookings do not qualify for the free stopover benefit. This is one reason round-trip awards can offer better value than two one-ways.

Aeroplan has improved its online multi-city search tool, and some stopover itineraries can now be booked online. However, complex routings may still require a phone call. When calling, clearly state you want a round-trip award with a stopover and specify your stopover city and dates.

The best stopover cities are Star Alliance hub cities with strong business class availability: Istanbul (Turkish Airlines hub), Zurich (SWISS hub), Vienna (Austrian hub), Singapore (Singapore Airlines hub), and Tokyo (ANA hub). These hubs offer the most consistent saver-level award space.

The Aeroplan stopover is one of the most powerful tools in the travel rewards playbook. By adding a free stopover to a round-trip business class award, you can explore two world-class cities for the points cost of visiting one. It requires planning, patience at the 355-day booking window, and comfort with Star Alliance routing rules, but the payoff is extraordinary: 10-15 cents per point in value on experiences that would cost thousands in cash. For the FI community member who has mastered basic award bookings and is ready to level up, this is the redemption that changes how you think about travel.

The Hidden Risks of Building Your Own Delivery Fleet as a Growing Business

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Building an in-house delivery fleet may seem like a smart way to reduce costs and gain greater control, but these challenges can quickly impact profitability.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

The invoices from the regional carrier keep getting bigger. For the owner of a growing home furnishings retailer, delivery has become one of the largest expenses outside of inventory, and the complaints that come with it — missed windows, damaged pieces, vague updates — still land on her desk.

So she runs the numbers on three cargo vans and three full-time drivers. On paper, the savings look obvious. What the spreadsheet leaves out is that she is not just buying vans. She is starting a transportation operation inside a company built to sell furniture.

The case for in-house delivery is legitimate. You decide when orders go out and how customers are treated at the door. You depend less on a third party’s capacity during busy weeks, you can coordinate dispatch directly with inventory, and at sufficient volume, you may lower your cost per delivery.

The catch is that an outside carrier bundles its costs into a price per stop. Once you own the operation, those costs scatter. Maintenance appears in repair invoices, insurance in renewal paperwork, depreciation on the balance sheet and recruiting and supervision both in payroll and in the owner’s calendar. Vehicles also sit idle on slow days and break down on busy ones.

In-house delivery is not automatically cheaper. Build a fully loaded cost per delivery that includes idle time, downtime, fuel, insurance, depreciation and management hours, then compare it with outsourced pricing across slow weeks as well as peak ones. A model built on your best month will flatter the fleet.

The same principle applies to broader ecommerce operations. Growing order volumes require companies to reconsider operational processes, technology investments and cost structures.

With two vans, the owner knows who drove which route and when the oil was last changed. That informality works, which is why it tends to persist long after it should be replaced.

At 10, 20 or 50 vehicles, decisions that once lived in one person’s head need to live in a system. Assign ownership for scheduling, maintenance, driver qualification files and performance. In a smaller operation, that may be one capable operations manager, but it must be a named role. Then track a short list of numbers: on-time rate, cost per delivery, vehicle downtime and incidents. Without them, you are managing by anecdote.

This transition reflects a broader challenge of business expansion. Building systems and teams that scale successfully emphasizes the importance of repeatable processes, clear responsibilities and infrastructure that can support increasing operational demands.

A driver is often the only person from your company a customer meets, and also the person operating a heavy vehicle on public roads with your inventory inside. Informal hiring does not scale. A repeatable process does.

Define written qualification criteria, including license status, driving history review where permitted and experience suited to your vehicles, and apply them to every candidate. Onboarding should cover cargo height and clearance, securing loads, customer handoffs and what to do when something goes wrong.

Put your standards for company vehicle use in writing, including phone use, personal use and defect reporting. Some vehicle sizes and operations trigger additional federal or local requirements, so verify what applies to your fleet.

Imagine a company that advertises tight two-hour windows and stacks routes to hit them. It works until a customer isn’t home or a freeway slows. Now the driver is behind, and the easiest way to recover is to speed up, skip a break or glance at a phone.

Fleet safety is partly a scheduling problem. If your routes only work when nothing goes wrong, you have designed in pressure. A few practical fixes help:

Tight windows can remain a selling point where routes are dense and predictable.

Reliable delivery also depends on operational consistency. Efficient workflows and well-organized teams help businesses maintain service standards as demand increases.

Relying on drivers to notice mechanical problems works for a few vehicles. It fails once drivers rotate through different vans and each assumes someone else reported the strange noise.

Set preventive service intervals based on manufacturer guidance and actual use. Require a short pre-trip inspection and make defect reporting penalty-free. Keep records in one place so patterns become visible, such as a van that keeps returning to the shop. Decide in advance that a vehicle with a safety-related problem comes out of service, even on a heavy day.

Then plan for downtime. A spare vehicle, a rental arrangement or an outside carrier on standby keeps one broken van from becoming a week of broken promises. Customers experience your maintenance program as reliability.

Most owners picture an accident as a repair bill. The real cost is wider: employee injuries, injuries to other road users, vehicle replacement, insurance claims and possible premium changes, delayed orders, lost productivity, administrative work and reputational damage. Depending on the circumstances, it can also raise legal questions, which is one reason consistent records matter.

The human side deserves honest attention. Delivery vans and box trucks are often heavier and taller than the passenger cars around them, and the injuries associated with commercial vehicle collisions, including head trauma, spinal damage and fractures, can change someone’s life and disrupt a business for a long time.

A one-page accident procedure, reviewed with every driver, should answer a few questions:

A business that outsources delivery pushes much of its transportation risk onto the carrier. Once you operate your own vehicles, that risk is yours to evaluate.

Work with a qualified broker to review limits, exclusions, covered drivers and how cargo is treated. Pay particular attention to the difference between company-owned vehicles and employees’ personal vehicles used for business, since those situations are often treated differently. Requirements and policy terms vary by jurisdiction and insurer, so confirm the details for your own operation.

Insurance also cannot replace good systems. Coverage helps absorb a loss. It does nothing to prevent the missed inspection, the rushed route or the untrained hire.

Sustainable growth requires systems that can operate consistently without constant intervention from the business owner. This principle is explored in an Entrepreneur article about building repeatable systems for long-term business growth, highlighting the importance of documenting, evaluating and continuously improving business processes.

Owning vehicles buys control, but control only pays off through discipline: clear ownership, consistent hiring, realistic schedules, preventive maintenance, an accident plan and a sober view of cost. The businesses that succeed with in-house delivery do so because they built a system around their vans, not because they bought good ones.

The invoices from the regional carrier keep getting bigger. For the owner of a growing home furnishings retailer, delivery has become one of the largest expenses outside of inventory, and the complaints that come with it — missed windows, damaged pieces, vague updates — still land on her desk.

So she runs the numbers on three cargo vans and three full-time drivers. On paper, the savings look obvious. What the spreadsheet leaves out is that she is not just buying vans. She is starting a transportation operation inside a company built to sell furniture.

The case for in-house delivery is legitimate. You decide when orders go out and how customers are treated at the door. You depend less on a third party’s capacity during busy weeks, you can coordinate dispatch directly with inventory, and at sufficient volume, you may lower your cost per delivery.
